It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.

I worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.

Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.

Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.

It’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. So, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.

I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.

Like, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. And it happens fast. Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.

I mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.

But emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.

Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.

That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.

And then I start thinking I’m not being a good husband or dad, which… I don’t know, it just keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unwanted is a good word for it. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like just focus on the code in front of me, and then one concern kind of grabs onto another. Like I’ll think, okay, what if I’m not doing this right, and then suddenly I’m picturing a bad review, and then losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to think about it. Even when I try to breathe through it, like during a workout or something, it just keeps running ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts hit, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make the anxiety go down?\n\nPatient: Um… over and over? I’m not sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes try to distract myself or check things again, but I don’t know if it’s like a “thing” I do, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I might reread an email a few times before sending it, but then again, everyone does that, right? Especially in tech, because one wrong word and suddenly someone thinks you don’t know what you’re talking about—well, not one word, but you know what I mean. I can kind of get stuck trying to make sure I didn’t miss something.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there’s a specific ritual or anything. It’s more like I get caught in my head and then I’m trying to feel better somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single event, exactly. It’s been more like… about nine months now? Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up, and then at home my wife and I started arguing more regularly.\n\nI mean, the work thing—those projects always sound temporary, but then they just take over everything, and everyone’s acting like it’s fine because “that’s tech,” you know? And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. And I guess I don’t always track my mood very well—I track work tasks better than my own head, which is probably not great. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.\n\nI worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.\n\nSo… no, it’s not just work and home tension anymore. It feels like everything piles on at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. But yeah. Most days.\n\nDoctor: When you reread emails or check things again, does it feel like you have to do it until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s “just right,” exactly. I mean, I do reread emails, but part of that is just… email is such a weird format, you know? Like one sentence can sound normal to me and then I imagine the other person reading it as passive-aggressive, and suddenly I’m changing “sure” to “sounds good” like it matters.\n\nBut I don’t know if it has that… feeling you’re describing. Like, I don’t think I’m aiming for some perfect click in my head. It’s more that I don’t trust my first pass, maybe. Or I get caught up in checking it and then realize I’ve spent way too long on something simple. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. If home felt better, then I’d start obsessing over whether I’m slipping at work. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F41.1","F43.23","F32.9","F33.9","F42.9"],"note":"","is_final":false}

It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / computer_occupation / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. I mean, I’ve had days where I feel kind of heavy or worn out, but then I’ll get distracted by work or something at home, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day.

Sorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. The last two weeks are hard to sort in my head because every day sort of blends into the next—emails, dishes, the same half-finished stuff around the house. But “sad most of the day, most days”… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that way.

I’ll be trying to do something normal, like just focus on the code in front of me, and then one concern kind of grabs onto another. Like I’ll think, okay, what if I’m not doing this right, and then suddenly I’m picturing a bad review, and then losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to think about it. And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. If home felt better, then I’d start obsessing over whether I’m slipping at work. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. I mean, I’ve had days where I feel kind of heavy or worn out, but then I’ll get distracted by work or something at home, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. The last two weeks are hard to sort in my head because every day sort of blends into the next—emails, dishes, the same half-finished stuff around the house. But “sad most of the day, most days”… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.

Most nights I’m exhausted when I get into bed, like I’m physically ready to pass out, but then my brain decides that’s the perfect time to start going through everything—work stuff, money, whether I said something wrong to my wife. And then it kind of branches out, like one thought turns into five.

Sometimes I do fall asleep, but then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and I’m immediately already rehearsing tomorrow’s problems. Like before I’m even fully awake, I’m thinking through emails I need to send or what might go wrong. Then I just lie there. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I can’t seem to shut my brain off. Even little things turn into this whole chain of “you’re messing this up too.”\n\nI work on computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot, but lately I keep thinking I’m slipping at work, and then I come home and see some half-done project and it’s like proof that I’m failing there too. And then I start thinking I’m not being a good husband or dad, which… I don’t know, it just keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unwanted is a good word for it. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like just focus on the code in front of me, and then one concern kind of grabs onto another. Like I’ll think, okay, what if I’m not doing this right, and then suddenly I’m picturing a bad review, and then losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to think about it. Even when I try to breathe through it, like during a workout or something, it just keeps running ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts hit, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make the anxiety go down?\n\nPatient: Um… over and over? I’m not sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes try to distract myself or check things again, but I don’t know if it’s like a “thing” I do, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I might reread an email a few times before sending it, but then again, everyone does that, right? It’s more like I get caught in my head and then I’m trying to feel better somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single event, exactly. It’s been more like… about nine months now? Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up, and then at home my wife and I started arguing more regularly.\n\nI mean, the work thing—those projects always sound temporary, but then they just take over everything, and everyone’s acting like it’s fine because “that’s tech,” you know? And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. And I guess I don’t always track my mood very well—I track work tasks better than my own head, which is probably not great. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.\n\nI worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.\n\nSo… no, it’s not just work and home tension anymore. It feels like everything piles on at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. Even when I try to breathe through it, like during a workout or something, it just keeps running ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts hit, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make the anxiety go down?\n\nPatient: Um… over and over? I’m not sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes try to distract myself or check things again, but I don’t know if it’s like a “thing” I do, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I might reread an email a few times before sending it, but then again, everyone does that, right? Especially in tech, because one wrong word and suddenly someone thinks you don’t know what you’re talking about—well, not one word, but you know what I mean. I can kind of get stuck trying to make sure I didn’t miss something.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there’s a specific ritual or anything. It’s more like I get caught in my head and then I’m trying to feel better somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single event, exactly. It’s been more like… about nine months now? Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up, and then at home my wife and I started arguing more regularly.\n\nI mean, the work thing—those projects always sound temporary, but then they just take over everything, and everyone’s acting like it’s fine because “that’s tech,” you know? And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. And I guess I don’t always track my mood very well—I track work tasks better than my own head, which is probably not great. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.\n\nI worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.\n\nSo… no, it’s not just work and home tension anymore. It feels like everything piles on at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. But yeah. Most days.\n\nDoctor: When you reread emails or check things again, does it feel like you have to do it until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s “just right,” exactly. I mean, I do reread emails, but part of that is just… email is such a weird format, you know? Like one sentence can sound normal to me and then I imagine the other person reading it as passive-aggressive, and suddenly I’m changing “sure” to “sounds good” like it matters.\n\nBut I don’t know if it has that… feeling you’re describing. Like, I don’t think I’m aiming for some perfect click in my head. It’s more that I don’t trust my first pass, maybe. Or I get caught up in checking it and then realize I’ve spent way too long on something simple. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. If home felt better, then I’d start obsessing over whether I’m slipping at work. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. I mean, I’ve had days where I feel kind of heavy or worn out, but then I’ll get distracted by work or something at home, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. The last two weeks are hard to sort in my head because every day sort of blends into the next—emails, dishes, the same half-finished stuff around the house. But “sad most of the day, most days”… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted when I get into bed, like I’m physically ready to pass out, but then my brain decides that’s the perfect time to start going through everything—work stuff, money, whether I said something wrong to my wife. And then it kind of branches out, like one thought turns into five.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and I’m immediately already rehearsing tomorrow’s problems. Like before I’m even fully awake, I’m thinking through emails I need to send or what might go wrong. Then I just lie there. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if that’s the same as “fatigued,” you know?\n\nLike yesterday I sat down after work and meant to just look at one thing on my phone, and then I kind of lost half an hour, but that could just be… I don’t know, normal adult life? I always picture “fatigued” as something more medical, like when my dad had mono years ago and couldn’t get off the couch. I’m not like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed being tired, but whether I’m “easily fatigued,” I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. But yeah. Most days.\n\nDoctor: When you reread emails or check things again, does it feel like you have to do it until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s “just right,” exactly. I mean, I do reread emails, but part of that is just… email is such a weird format, you know? Like one sentence can sound normal to me and then I imagine the other person reading it as passive-aggressive, and suddenly I’m changing “sure” to “sounds good” like it matters.\n\nBut I don’t know if it has that… feeling you’re describing. Like, I don’t think I’m aiming for some perfect click in my head. It’s more that I don’t trust my first pass, maybe. Or I get caught up in checking it and then realize I’ve spent way too long on something simple. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. I always picture “fatigued” as something more medical, like when my dad had mono years ago and couldn’t get off the couch. I’m not like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed being tired, but whether I’m “easily fatigued,” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, it’s embarrassing because my job is basically concentrating for a living.\n\nLike in sprint planning or code reviews, I’ll be looking at a design note and realize I’ve read the same paragraph three or four times and I still didn’t catch what it was saying. And then someone asks about it and I’m sort of scrambling because part of my brain wasn’t even there—it was off thinking about whether I forgot something at home, or replaying how badly the next argument is going to go.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because it’s not like the note is complicated, exactly. Well, sometimes it is, but… yeah. I’m having trouble staying locked in.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore this started about nine months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed for at least two weeks?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 35 / computer_occupation / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like when work was bad or after certain life stuff, but I don’t know if I’d call it “depressed for two weeks.” That word feels pretty specific.

In college I remember being kind of miserable during finals, but everyone was miserable then, and I was also staying up too late and eating terrible food, so it’s hard to separate. And there were times after projects ended where I felt sort of flat, but again, I don’t know if that counts.

So I guess I don’t have a clear “yes, that happened” answer. I’d have to think about it more.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.23, F32.9, F33.9, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I can’t seem to shut my brain off. It’s like I’m worrying about everything at the same time—my marriage, work, money, the house stuff I keep starting and not finishing. Even little things turn into this whole chain of “you’re messing this up too.”\n\nI work on computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot, but lately I keep thinking I’m slipping at work, and then I come home and see some half-done project and it’s like proof that I’m failing there too. And then I start thinking I’m not being a good husband or dad, which… I don’t know, it just keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unwanted is a good word for it. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like just focus on the code in front of me, and then one concern kind of grabs onto another. Like I’ll think, okay, what if I’m not doing this right, and then suddenly I’m picturing a bad review, and then losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to think about it. Even when I try to breathe through it, like during a workout or something, it just keeps running ahead of me.\n\nDoctor: When these thoughts hit, do you find yourself doing anything over and over to make the anxiety go down?\n\nPatient: Um… over and over? I’m not sure. I mean, I’ll sometimes try to distract myself or check things again, but I don’t know if it’s like a “thing” I do, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I might reread an email a few times before sending it, but then again, everyone does that, right? Especially in tech, because one wrong word and suddenly someone thinks you don’t know what you’re talking about—well, not one word, but you know what I mean. I can kind of get stuck trying to make sure I didn’t miss something.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d say there’s a specific ritual or anything. It’s more like I get caught in my head and then I’m trying to feel better somehow.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was one single event, exactly. It’s been more like… about nine months now? Around when the cloud migration at work really ramped up, and then at home my wife and I started arguing more regularly.\n\nI mean, the work thing—those projects always sound temporary, but then they just take over everything, and everyone’s acting like it’s fine because “that’s tech,” you know? And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. And I guess I don’t always track my mood very well—I track work tasks better than my own head, which is probably not great. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.\n\nI worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.\n\nSo… no, it’s not just work and home tension anymore. It feels like everything piles on at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. But yeah. Most days.\n\nDoctor: When you reread emails or check things again, does it feel like you have to do it until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s “just right,” exactly. I mean, I do reread emails, but part of that is just… email is such a weird format, you know? Like one sentence can sound normal to me and then I imagine the other person reading it as passive-aggressive, and suddenly I’m changing “sure” to “sounds good” like it matters.\n\nBut I don’t know if it has that… feeling you’re describing. Like, I don’t think I’m aiming for some perfect click in my head. It’s more that I don’t trust my first pass, maybe. Or I get caught up in checking it and then realize I’ve spent way too long on something simple. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. If home felt better, then I’d start obsessing over whether I’m slipping at work. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. I mean, I’ve had days where I feel kind of heavy or worn out, but then I’ll get distracted by work or something at home, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. The last two weeks are hard to sort in my head because every day sort of blends into the next—emails, dishes, the same half-finished stuff around the house. But “sad most of the day, most days”… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted when I get into bed, like I’m physically ready to pass out, but then my brain decides that’s the perfect time to start going through everything—work stuff, money, whether I said something wrong to my wife. And then it kind of branches out, like one thought turns into five.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and I’m immediately already rehearsing tomorrow’s problems. Like before I’m even fully awake, I’m thinking through emails I need to send or what might go wrong. Then I just lie there. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if that’s the same as “fatigued,” you know?\n\nLike yesterday I sat down after work and meant to just look at one thing on my phone, and then I kind of lost half an hour, but that could just be… I don’t know, normal adult life? I always picture “fatigued” as something more medical, like when my dad had mono years ago and couldn’t get off the couch. I’m not like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed being tired, but whether I’m “easily fatigued,” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, it’s embarrassing because my job is basically concentrating for a living.\n\nLike in sprint planning or code reviews, I’ll be looking at a design note and realize I’ve read the same paragraph three or four times and I still didn’t catch what it was saying. And then someone asks about it and I’m sort of scrambling because part of my brain wasn’t even there—it was off thinking about whether I forgot something at home, or replaying how badly the next argument is going to go.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because it’s not like the note is complicated, exactly. Well, sometimes it is, but… yeah. I’m having trouble staying locked in.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about nine months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like when work was bad or after certain life stuff, but I don’t know if I’d call it “depressed for two weeks.” That word feels pretty specific.\n\nIn college I remember being kind of miserable during finals, but everyone was miserable then, and I was also staying up too late and eating terrible food, so it’s hard to separate. And there were times after projects ended where I felt sort of flat, but again, I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clear “yes, that happened” answer. I’d have to think about it more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even little things turn into this whole chain of “you’re messing this up too.”\n\nI work on computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot, but lately I keep thinking I’m slipping at work, and then I come home and see some half-done project and it’s like proof that I’m failing there too. And then I start thinking I’m not being a good husband or dad, which… I don’t know, it just keeps looping.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel intrusive and unwanted, like they pop into your mind even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… unwanted is a good word for it. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like just focus on the code in front of me, and then one concern kind of grabs onto another. Like I’ll think, okay, what if I’m not doing this right, and then suddenly I’m picturing a bad review, and then losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m sitting there choosing to think about it. And then I’d come home already wound up, and if there was tension there too, it just kind of… kept building. So yeah, not one dramatic thing, but that stretch is when it really started getting bad.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I’ve definitely felt worn down, but “down most days” is hard for me to say.\n\nLike, some days I’m just kind of flat, but then I’ll get busy with something and not really notice it. And I guess I don’t always track my mood very well—I track work tasks better than my own head, which is probably not great. So… maybe a little, but I’m not sure it’s most days.\n\nDoctor: When you worry like this, is it mostly about the work situation and tension at home, or do you find yourself worrying just as much about lots of other things too?\n\nPatient: It started with work and stuff at home, but now it’s… kind of everything. Like I can’t keep the tabs closed in my head.\n\nI worry about my marriage, obviously, and whether I’m slipping at work—like maybe people are noticing before I am. But then it jumps to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and not finishing. I was fixing this cabinet thing the other weekend and somehow that turned into me thinking, “Great, this is just another example of me dropping the ball,” and then suddenly I’m thinking I’m failing as a husband and a dad.\n\nSo… no, it’s not just work and home tension anymore. It feels like everything piles on at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly.\n\nI’ll try to tell myself, okay, just focus on the code in front of you, fix this one thing, don’t spiral. Or I’ll be at the gym and think, just breathe, just get through the workout. But one worry kind of grabs onto the next one.\n\nLike, I’ll think maybe I’m falling behind at work, and then suddenly I’m imagining a bad review, and then that turns into losing my job, and then I’m thinking my family would be worse off because of me. And it happens fast. I know it sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels like I’m already twenty steps down the road before I even realize I went there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense or keyed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like my shoulders are up around my ears and locked there.\n\nIt’s like I’m bracing for something, even when nothing is actually happening. Between meetings I’ll catch myself pacing around the room, or if I’m at chess club I’ll be tapping the pieces or shifting in my chair. Which is embarrassing, because chess is supposed to be this calm, thoughtful thing, and I’m sitting there like I drank six coffees.\n\nSo, yeah, sitting still feels really hard a lot of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying things less than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, maybe? It’s hard to separate “not enjoying” from just being distracted the whole time.\n\nLike, I still do things I usually like—chess, going to the gym, messing with little projects around the house—but sometimes I’m halfway through and realize I’m not really in it. But then again, I was never one of those people who’s like, wildly enthusiastic about hobbies, you know? Even in grad school I’d get obsessive about something for two weeks and then move on. I had this whole phase with brewing coffee, bought a scale and everything, and now the scale is in a drawer with random screws.\n\nSo… I’m not sure I’d say I enjoy things less exactly. I just don’t always feel very present for them.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I’m not sure. That word feels pretty big to me.\n\nI mean, I get frustrated and kind of tired of feeling like my brain won’t shut up, but I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless. It’s funny, because in programming “hopeless” is like when you look at some ancient codebase and think, who even wrote this, and then it turns out it was you six months ago. So I guess I’ve felt that version of it sometimes.\n\nBut emotionally… I don’t know. I haven’t really sat there thinking there’s no point or anything like that. I think I’m more just overwhelmed than hopeless.\n\nDoctor: Over the past six months or so, have you been worrying on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… yes, most days, pretty much.\n\nThat’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like I can’t just worry about one thing at a time—my marriage, whether I’m messing up at work, bills, the stuff around the house I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing, like, am I just failing as a husband and father?\n\nAnd I know some of it sounds ordinary, like everyone worries about bills or work. But it’s the way it all piles up at once. I’ll be trying to answer an email and suddenly I’m thinking about the leaky thing in the basement, and then whether my kid notices I’m distracted, and then—sorry, that’s not really the question. But yeah. Most days.\n\nDoctor: When you reread emails or check things again, does it feel like you have to do it until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure it’s “just right,” exactly. I mean, I do reread emails, but part of that is just… email is such a weird format, you know? Like one sentence can sound normal to me and then I imagine the other person reading it as passive-aggressive, and suddenly I’m changing “sure” to “sounds good” like it matters.\n\nBut I don’t know if it has that… feeling you’re describing. Like, I don’t think I’m aiming for some perfect click in my head. It’s more that I don’t trust my first pass, maybe. Or I get caught up in checking it and then realize I’ve spent way too long on something simple. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: If the problems at work and the tension at home were to settle down, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I’d like to think it would, but… honestly, I don’t think it would mostly go away.\n\nBecause it’s not just one problem. I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once—my marriage, work, bills, house projects I start and then don’t finish, and then it all turns into this bigger question of whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father.\n\nLike, if work calmed down, I’d probably just shift to the bills or the house. If home felt better, then I’d start obsessing over whether I’m slipping at work. It’s like whack-a-mole, except less fun and with more spreadsheets.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt sad or down most of the day on most days?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad most of the day. I mean, I’ve had days where I feel kind of heavy or worn out, but then I’ll get distracted by work or something at home, and it’s not like I’m sitting there crying all day.\n\nSorry, that’s probably not a clean answer. The last two weeks are hard to sort in my head because every day sort of blends into the next—emails, dishes, the same half-finished stuff around the house. But “sad most of the day, most days”… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah. Sleep has been… pretty bad, honestly.\n\nMost nights I’m exhausted when I get into bed, like I’m physically ready to pass out, but then my brain decides that’s the perfect time to start going through everything—work stuff, money, whether I said something wrong to my wife. And then it kind of branches out, like one thought turns into five.\n\nSometimes I do fall asleep, but then I wake up at like 3 or 4 in the morning and I’m immediately already rehearsing tomorrow’s problems. Like before I’m even fully awake, I’m thinking through emails I need to send or what might go wrong. Then I just lie there. So yes, definitely trouble sleeping.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I feel worn out a lot, but I don’t know if that’s the same as “fatigued,” you know?\n\nLike yesterday I sat down after work and meant to just look at one thing on my phone, and then I kind of lost half an hour, but that could just be… I don’t know, normal adult life? I always picture “fatigued” as something more medical, like when my dad had mono years ago and couldn’t get off the couch. I’m not like that.\n\nSo… I guess I’ve noticed being tired, but whether I’m “easily fatigued,” I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, it’s embarrassing because my job is basically concentrating for a living.\n\nLike in sprint planning or code reviews, I’ll be looking at a design note and realize I’ve read the same paragraph three or four times and I still didn’t catch what it was saying. And then someone asks about it and I’m sort of scrambling because part of my brain wasn’t even there—it was off thinking about whether I forgot something at home, or replaying how badly the next argument is going to go.\n\nAnd then I get annoyed with myself, because it’s not like the note is complicated, exactly. Well, sometimes it is, but… yeah. I’m having trouble staying locked in.\n\nDoctor: Before this started about nine months ago, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches before, like when work was bad or after certain life stuff, but I don’t know if I’d call it “depressed for two weeks.” That word feels pretty specific.\n\nIn college I remember being kind of miserable during finals, but everyone was miserable then, and I was also staying up too late and eating terrible food, so it’s hard to separate. And there were times after projects ended where I felt sort of flat, but again, I don’t know if that counts.\n\nSo I guess I don’t have a clear “yes, that happened” answer. I’d have to think about it more.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying even when there isn’t anything specific going wrong?\n\nPatient: Yeah. That’s kind of why I came in, honestly. It’s like even when nothing is actively on fire, my brain is still looking around for what I should be worried about.\n\nIt’ll be my marriage, or whether I’m slipping at work, or bills, or the house projects I keep starting and then dropping. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I’m like, maybe I’m just failing at all of it—as a husband, as a dad. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but in my head it feels very real.\n\nAnd it’s not always one clear problem. It’s more like… everything at once, all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. That’s a hard one to answer cleanly.\n\nI mean, I get really overwhelmed sometimes, and I’ll have these moments where I’m just exhausted by my own head, if that makes sense. Like, I’ll be sitting in traffic or trying to answer emails and suddenly everything feels heavier than it should. But “life isn’t worth living” feels like a really specific thing to say, and I don’t know if I’d put it that way.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling it. I guess I’m not sure. I’d have to think about what counts as that versus just feeling worn down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
